---

## Implementation Plan: Luma AMD Speedrun -- Elegant Compound Kernel Optimization

### Analysis Summary

After reading all current submissions, failed experiments, vault learnings, and competition constraints, here is what I see:

**The fundamental insight:** The three kernels have vastly different optimization profiles and expected ROI.

| Kernel | Gap | Nature of Gap | Achievable Improvement |
|--------|-----|---------------|----------------------|
| MoE | 1.07x | Tuning ceiling -- already using aiter's best ASM | 2-5% marginal |
| GEMM | 2.4x | Quant overhead is the only lever -- ASM GEMM is shared | 20-40% if quant is fused |
| MLA | 17x | Python dispatch + metadata overhead dominates | Unknown -- depends on whether C++ bridge works |

### What the evidence tells us

1. **MLA 4.3us leader is almost certainly calling CK ASM kernels directly from C++**, bypassing the entire Python `mla_decode_fwd` pipeline (metadata build, Python dispatch, reduce kernel). The Gemini squad's `submission_mla_cpp.py` attempted exactly this via `load_inline` linking to `aiter::asm_mla_decode_fwd` -- the right idea, but unclear if it linked successfully.

2. **GEMM's 2.4x gap is almost entirely `dynamic_mxfp4_quant` overhead.** The `gemm_a4w4` ASM kernel is shared between us and the leader. The leader's advantage is either (a) a fused quant-GEMM kernel, or (b) faster quantization. The HIP quant kernel works but its E8M0 rounding doesn't match aiter's exactly (the diagnostic submission showed scale mismatches).

3. **MoE is almost at ceiling.** The adaptive ksplit=4 + NT + sparse/dense routing is close to optimal. The remaining 7% gap likely comes from per-shape tuned CSV configs the leader found through exhaustive search.

---

### The Plan: Three Layers, Compound

Each layer's tooling and learnings feed the next.

#### Layer 1: Fix GEMM Quant Rounding (Highest Expected Value)

**Goal:** Replace `dynamic_mxfp4_quant` (Triton, ~10-13us) with a bit-accurate HIP C++ kernel (~3-5us). The current HIP kernel has wrong E8M0 rounding.

**Root cause of rounding mismatch:** The current HIP kernel uses `floorf(log2f(amax / 6.0f)) + 128.0f` to compute E8M0 scale bytes. But aiter's `dynamic_mxfp4_quant` uses IEEE 754 bitwise rounding: extract exponent bits, apply round-up-on-tie logic. The diagnostic in `submission.py` (the main GEMM file) confirmed scales differ by +/-1 in ~30% of groups.

**Implementation:**
1. Replace the `log2f` scale computation in the HIP kernel with bitwise IEEE 754 logic matching `_mxfp4_quant_op_precise` from `submission_breakthrough_v9.py` (lines 17-22):
   ```c
   // Extract exponent from IEEE 754 float
   unsigned int u32 = __float_as_uint(amax);
   int exponent = (u32 >> 23) & 0xFF;
   int round_up = ((u32 & 0x400000) != 0) && (((u32 & 0x3FFFFF) != 0) || (exponent > 0));
   int exp_val = round_up ? exponent + 1 : exponent;
   scale_byte = (unsigned char)(exp_val - 129 + 127);  // = exp_val - 2
   scale_factor = exp2f((float)(exp_val - 129));
   ```
2. Use `torch.utils.cpp_extension.load_inline` instead of raw amdclang++ (cleaner, PyTorch handles linking).
3. Validate with the same diagnostic pattern: compare `hip_scale == ref_scale_raw` for 100% match.
4. If HIP quant matches exactly, pipeline becomes: HIP quant (~3us) + e8m0_shuffle (~1us) + gemm_a4w4 (~15us) = ~19us total vs current ~23us.

**Expected improvement:** 23us -> ~17-19us (17-26% faster). Narrows gap from 2.4x to ~1.8x.

**Compound payoff:** The bit-accurate HIP quant kernel becomes a reusable building block. It can later be fused into a single-launch quant+GEMM kernel (Layer 3).

#### Layer 2: MLA C++ ASM Bridge (Highest Potential, Highest Risk)

**Goal:** Bypass the entire Python `mla_decode_fwd` pipeline by calling aiter's ASM kernels directly from C++ via `load_inline`.

**Why this is the right approach:** The 4.3us leader time proves the hardware CAN do MLA decode that fast. Our 74us includes ~50us of Python overhead (metadata build + Python-level dispatch). The ASM `.co` files at `/home/runner/aiter/hsa/gfx950/mla/` are the actual compute kernels -- they're fast enough.

**Implementation:**
1. Start from the Gemini squad's `submission_mla_cpp.py` pattern but fix the linking:
   - Discover aiter's C++ shared library path: `python -c "import aiter; print(aiter.__file__)"` then find the `.so` containing `asm_mla_decode_fwd`.
   - Alternative: use `ctypes.CDLL` to find the symbol at runtime, then pass the function pointer to our C++ extension.
2. Build a minimal C++ bridge that:
   - Takes Q, KV, indptrs, and config
   - Builds the metadata in C++ (bypassing Python's `get_mla_metadata_v1`)
   - Calls `asm_mla_decode_fwd` with the correct arguments
   - Returns the output tensor
3. Cache the compiled extension per session.
4. Routing: Use C++ bridge for all decode shapes, fall back to current Python path on failure.

**Key risks:**
- The `asm_mla_decode_fwd` symbol may not be exported, or its signature may differ from what the Gemini squad assumed.
- The metadata build is complex -- may need to replicate `get_mla_metadata_v1` logic in C++.
- If the ASM kernel uses internal state or context that's only set up by the Python wrapper, direct calls may crash.

**Mitigation:** Probe the runner environment first with a diagnostic submission that:
- Lists all exported symbols from aiter's `.so` files
- Prints the actual function signatures
- Tests a minimal C++ call with trivial inputs

**Expected improvement:** If successful, 74us -> 8-15us (5-9x faster). If the metadata can be cached in C++, possibly 4-8us.

**Compound payoff:** The C++ bridge pattern, once working for MLA, can be reused for any aiter kernel -- including MoE dispatch.

#### Layer 3: MoE Fine-Grained Shape Routing (Low Risk, Incremental)

**Goal:** Squeeze the remaining 7% from MoE by testing more env var combinations per-shape.

**Implementation:**
1. Add `AITER_BLOCK_M` routing: Currently the code only routes ksplit. Add block_m routing:
   - `estimated_m < 16`: ksplit=4, block_m=32
   - `16 <= estimated_m < 100`: ksplit=4, block_m=64
   - `estimated_m >= 100`: default (tuned CSV)
2. Test `doweight_stage1=True` specifically for shapes where it previously crashed (it may work for a subset).
3. If Layer 2's C++ bridge pattern works, apply it to MoE: bypass `fused_moe`'s Python dispatch and call the CK ASM kernel directly with pre-computed metadata.

**Expected improvement:** 155us -> 148-150us (3-5% faster). Marginal but moves ranking.

---

### Execution Sequence (Compound Order)

```
Step 1: GEMM Quant Fix (Layer 1)
  - Fix E8M0 rounding in HIP kernel
  - Validate bit-accuracy
  - Submit and benchmark
  ~2 hours, high confidence

Step 2: MLA Probe Submission (Layer 2 prerequisite)
  - Diagnostic submission that maps aiter's C++ symbols
  - Identifies exact function signatures and .so paths
  - No performance goal, just intelligence gathering
  ~1 hour

Step 3: MLA C++ Bridge (Layer 2)
  - Build load_inline extension using probe results
  - Test with smallest shape first (bs=4, kv=1k)
  - Progressively enable for larger shapes
  ~3-4 hours, medium confidence

Step 4: MoE Tuning (Layer 3)
  - Fine-grained block_m routing
  - Only if Steps 1-3 are complete
  ~1 hour, high confidence
```

### What NOT to Pursue (Dead Ends)

1. **Custom HIP attention kernels** -- Proven 19-100x regression. Writing a competitive attention kernel from scratch requires months of AMD GPU expertise.
2. **CUDA/HIP graphs** -- Always regress due to `copy_()` overhead.
3. **Triton FlashDecoding** -- Matches aiter's ~130us, doesn't beat it. The Triton JIT dispatch itself is ~130us.
4. **Fused Triton quant+GEMM** -- Phase 6 proved standard Triton can't beat aiter's persistent kernel. The Helion-generated code is a starting point but won't beat the ASM.
5. **Custom Triton MLA with head_dim=576** -- VGPR pressure makes this impossible without split-D, and split-D adds bandwidth overhead that nullifies the dispatch savings.

     1→import sys
     2→import torch
     3→import triton
     4→import triton.language as tl
     5→import aiter
     6→from task import input_t, output_t
     7→from aiter import dtypes, QuantType
     8→from aiter.ops.triton.quant import dynamic_mxfp4_quant
     9→from aiter.utility.fp4_utils import e8m0_shuffle
    10→
    11→# 1. Exact bit-level rounding from aiter C++ backend
    12→@triton.jit
    13→def _mxfp4_quant_op_precise(x, BLOCK_SIZE_N, BLOCK_SIZE_M, MXFP4_QUANT_BLOCK_SIZE):
    14→    NUM_QUANT_BLOCKS: tl.constexpr = BLOCK_SIZE_N // MXFP4_QUANT_BLOCK_SIZE
    15→    x_grouped = tl.reshape(x, [BLOCK_SIZE_M, NUM_QUANT_BLOCKS, MXFP4_QUANT_BLOCK_SIZE])
    16→    amax = tl.max(tl.abs(x_grouped), axis=2)
    17→    u32 = amax.to(tl.int32, bitcast=True)
    18→    exponent = ((u32 >> 23) & 0xFF)
    19→    round_case = ((u32 & 0x400000) != 0) & (((u32 & 0x3FFFFF) != 0) | (exponent > 0))
    20→    exp_val = tl.where(round_case, exponent + 1, exponent)
    21→    scale_unbiased = exp_val - 129
    22→    bs_e8m0 = (scale_unbiased + 127).to(tl.uint8)
    23→    quant_scale_inv = tl.exp2(-scale_unbiased.to(tl.float32))
    24→    qx = x_grouped * quant_scale_inv[:, :, None]
    25→    sign = (qx < 0).to(tl.int32)
    26→    x_abs = tl.abs(qx)
    27→    mag = tl.where(x_abs < 0.25, 0,
    28→          tl.where(x_abs < 0.75, 1,
    29→          tl.where(x_abs < 1.25, 2,
    30→          tl.where(x_abs < 1.75, 3,
    31→          tl.where(x_abs < 2.5,  4,
    32→          tl.where(x_abs < 3.5,  5,
    33→          tl.where(x_abs < 5.0,  6, 7)))))))
    34→    codes = (sign << 3) | mag
    35→    codes_flat = tl.reshape(codes, [BLOCK_SIZE_M, BLOCK_SIZE_N])
    36→    idx_k = tl.arange(0, BLOCK_SIZE_N)
    37→    shift = tl.where(idx_k % 2 == 1, 4, 0)
    38→    shifted = (codes_flat & 0xF) << shift[None, :]
    39→    x_fp4 = tl.sum(tl.reshape(shifted, [BLOCK_SIZE_M, BLOCK_SIZE_N // 2, 2]), axis=2).to(tl.uint8)
    40→    return x_fp4, tl.reshape(bs_e8m0, [BLOCK_SIZE_M, NUM_QUANT_BLOCKS])
    41→
    42→@triton.jit
    43→def _fused_gemm_kernel_precise(
    44→    A_ptr, B_ptr, C_ptr, B_scale_ptr,
    45→    M, N, K,
    46→    stride_am, stride_ak,
    47→    stride_bk, stride_bn,
    48→    stride_cm, stride_cn,
    49→    stride_bs_n, stride_bs_k,
    50→    BLOCK_M: tl.constexpr, BLOCK_N: tl.constexpr, BLOCK_K: tl.constexpr,
    51→):
    52→    pid = tl.program_id(0)
    53→    total_blocks_M = tl.cdiv(M, BLOCK_M)
    54→    pid_m = pid % total_blocks_M
    55→    pid_n = pid // total_blocks_M
    56→    offs_m = pid_m * BLOCK_M + tl.arange(0, BLOCK_M)
    57→    offs_n = pid_n * BLOCK_N + tl.arange(0, BLOCK_N)
    58→    mask_m = offs_m[:, None] < M
    59→    mask_n = offs_n[None, :] < N
    60→    acc = tl.zeros([BLOCK_M, BLOCK_N], dtype=tl.float32)

    24→
    25→#define BLOCK 256
    26→#define GROUP_SIZE 32
    27→
    28→__global__ void mxfp4_quant_kernel(

    30→    unsigned char* __restrict__ A_q,
    31→    unsigned char* __restrict__ A_scale,

    33→{
    34→    const int LANES = 16;
    35→    const int num_groups_per_row = K / GROUP_SIZE;
    36→    const int total_groups = M * num_groups_per_row;
    37→
    38→    int global_tid = blockIdx.x * BLOCK + threadIdx.x;
    39→    int group_idx = global_tid / LANES;
    40→    int lane = global_tid % LANES;
    41→
    42→    if (group_idx >= total_groups) return;
    43→
    44→    int row = group_idx / num_groups_per_row;
    45→    int grp = group_idx % num_groups_per_row;
    46→
    47→    int base = row * K + grp * GROUP_SIZE;
    48→    float v0 = __bfloat162float(A[base + lane * 2]);
    49→    float v1 = __bfloat162float(A[base + lane * 2 + 1]);
    50→
    51→    __shared__ float red[BLOCK];
    52→    int local_group = threadIdx.x / LANES;
    53→    float local_max = fmaxf(fabsf(v0), fabsf(v1));
    54→    red[threadIdx.x] = local_max;
    55→    __syncthreads();
    56→
    57→    int group_base = local_group * LANES;
    58→    for (int stride = LANES / 2; stride > 0; stride >>= 1) {
    59→        if (lane < stride)
    60→            red[group_base + lane] = fmaxf(red[group_base + lane],
    61→                                            red[group_base + lane + stride]);
    62→        __syncthreads();
    63→    }
    64→    float group_max = red[group_base];
    65→    __syncthreads();
    66→
    67→    unsigned char scale_byte;
    68→    float scale_factor;
    69→
    70→    if (group_max > 0.0f) {

    72→        log2_val = fminf(fmaxf(log2_val, 0.0f), 254.0f);
    73→        scale_byte = (unsigned char)log2_val;
    74→        scale_factor = exp2f((float)scale_byte - 127.0f);

    81→    float n1 = (scale_factor > 0.0f) ? v1 / scale_factor : 0.0f;
    82→
    83→    auto encode_fp4 = [](float x) -> unsigned char {

    85→        float ax = fabsf(x);

    87→        if      (ax < 0.25f) mag = 0;
    88→        else if (ax < 0.75f) mag = 1;
    89→        else if (ax < 1.25f) mag = 2;
    90→        else if (ax < 1.75f) mag = 3;
    91→        else if (ax < 2.5f)  mag = 4;
    92→        else if (ax < 3.5f)  mag = 5;
    93→        else if (ax < 5.0f)  mag = 6;
    94→        else                  mag = 7;
    95→        return (unsigned char)((sign << 3) | mag);
    96→    };
    97→
    98→    unsigned char fp4_0 = encode_fp4(n0);

   100→    unsigned char packed = (fp4_1 << 4) | (fp4_0 & 0x0F);

     1→# Luma AMD Speedrun: Phase 15 — Compound Custom Kernels
     2→
     3→## Context
     4→
     5→We're at the Python dispatch ceiling across all three kernels. 14 phases of optimization have exhausted every Python-level API path. The only way forward is custom kernels compiled on the runner itself.
     6→
     7→**Critical prior learnings (vault + skills):**
     8→- amdclang++ → .so → ctypes pipeline WORKS (confirmed 2026-03-15)
     9→- Naive HIP attention kernel = **19-100x REGRESSION** — kernel quality >> dispatch overhead
    10→- GEMM HIP quant kernel works but has **wrong E8M0 rounding** (±1 scale mismatch in ~30% of groups)
    11→- Bit-accurate E8M0 logic EXISTS in `submission_breakthrough_v9.py` (IEEE 754 bitwise)
    12→- VGPR budget: `acc[32,512]` fp32 = 256 VGPRs = entire budget. Must use split-D or smaller tiles.
    13→- Helion generates correct `tl.dot_scaled` patterns for MXFP4 GEMM
    14→
    15→### Current Standings (Phase 14 best)
    16→
    17→| Kernel | Our Time | Leader | Gap | Status |
    18→|--------|----------|--------|-----|--------|
    19→| MLA | 74.3 µs | 4.3 µs | 17x | Phase 14 OR routing + 3D matmul |
    20→| GEMM | ~23 µs | 9.7 µs | 2.4x | dynamic_mxfp4_quant + gemm_a4w4 |
    21→| MoE | ~155 µs | 145 µs | 1.07x | Adaptive KSPLIT (API ceiling) |
    22→
    23→## Strategy: Compound Layers (Each Enables the Next)
    24→
    25→### Layer 1: GEMM — Fix HIP Quant Rounding (HIGH confidence, ~17-26% improvement)
    26→
    27→**Problem:** The existing HIP C++ quant kernel uses `floorf(log2f(amax / 6.0f)) + 128.0f` which produces scale bytes ±1 off from aiter's reference. This makes the entire HIP quant output unusable for `gemm_a4w4`.
    28→
    29→**Solution:** Port the bit-accurate E8M0 logic from `submission_breakthrough_v9.py:17-22` to HIP C++:
    30→```c
    31→// IEEE 754 bitwise: extract exponent, round-up-on-tie
    32→unsigned int u32 = __float_as_uint(group_max);
    33→int exponent = (u32 >> 23) & 0xFF;
    34→int round_up = ((u32 & 0x400000) != 0) && (((u32 & 0x3FFFFF) != 0) || (exponent > 0));
    35→int exp_val = round_up ? exponent + 1 : exponent;
    36→scale_byte = (unsigned char)(exp_val - 129 + 127);  // = exp_val - 2
    37→scale_factor = exp2f((float)(exp_val - 129));
    38→```
    39→
    40→**Pipeline:** HIP quant (~3-5µs) → e8m0_shuffle (~1µs) → gemm_a4w4 ASM (~15µs) = **~19-21µs**
    41→
    42→**Why this is compound:** Once HIP quant matches bit-perfectly, it becomes a reusable building block for Layer 3 (fused quant+GEMM). And the compile+load pattern is battle-tested for Layer 2.
    43→
    44→### Layer 2: MLA — C++ Bridge to aiter ASM (MEDIUM confidence, potentially 5-10x improvement)
    45→
    46→**Problem:** 74µs MLA includes ~50µs of Python overhead (metadata build + dispatch). The ASM `.co` kernels at `/home/runner/aiter/hsa/gfx950/mla/` run in ~4-10µs — the overhead IS the bottleneck.
    47→
    48→**Solution:** Use `torch.utils.cpp_extension.load_inline` to build a C++ bridge that calls aiter's ASM kernels directly, bypassing the Python pipeline.
    49→
    50→**Step 2a — Probe submission (intelligence gathering):**
    51→Submit a diagnostic that maps aiter's exported C++ symbols:
    52→- `python -c "import aiter; print(aiter.__file__)"` → find .so paths
    53→- `nm -D` on the .so files → list exported symbols containing "mla"
    54→- Print `torch.ops.aiter` registered ops
    55→- List `/home/runner/aiter/hsa/gfx950/mla/*.co` filenames
    56→
    57→**Step 2b — Minimal C++ bridge:**
    58→If symbols are exported, build a `load_inline` extension that:
    59→1. Takes Q, KV, metadata tensors as `torch::Tensor`
    60→2. Calls the ASM function via `torch::ops::aiter::*` or direct symbol
    61→3. Returns output — single C++ function call, no Python overhead
    62→
    63→**Step 2c — Full integration with fallback:**
    64→Route through C++ bridge for decode shapes, fall back to Python aiter for errors.
    65→
    66→**Why NOT another naive HIP kernel:** The vault experiment proved a naive kernel is 19-100x slower. We should leverage aiter's highly-optimized ASM rather than compete with it. The elegant approach: remove the overhead around the fast kernel, don't rewrite the fast kernel.
    67→
    68→### Layer 3: GEMM — Fused Quant+GEMM (LOWER confidence, requires Layer 1)
    69→
    70→**Only attempt if Layer 1 succeeds and time permits.** Fuse the HIP quant kernel with the GEMM into a single launch, eliminating the intermediate A_q buffer and the e8m0_shuffle call.

    74→- **Path B:** HIP C++ kernel using `__builtin_amdgcn_mfma_f32_32x32x64_fp4` (harder but potentially competitive)

    76→### Layer 4: MoE — Fine Block_M Routing (LOW risk, incremental)
    77→
    78→Only if Layers 1-2 complete. Add per-shape `AITER_BLOCK_M` routing based on `estimated_m`:

    81→- `estimated_m ≥ 100`: default CSV

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
The "no fallbacks" philosophy is crucial for competitive kernel optimization. Fallback paths create a comfort zone that prevents you from ever fixing the fast path. In GPU kernel development, the difference between a working custom kernel and "falling back to the library" is often the difference between winning and losing — you never debug what you never run.

The only way forward is custom kernels compiled on the runner itself.\n\n**Critical prior learnings (vault + skills):**\n- amdclang++ → .so → ctypes pipeline WORKS (confirmed 2026-03-15)\n- Naive HIP attention kernel = **19-100x REGRESSION** — kernel quality >> dispatch overhead\n- GEMM HIP quant kernel works but has **wrong E8M0 rounding** (±1 scale mismatch in ~30% of groups)\n- Bit-accurate E8M0 logic EXISTS in `submission_breakthrough_v9.py` (IEEE 754 bitwise)\n- VGPR budget: `acc[32,512]` fp32 = 256 VGPRs = entire budget. Must use split-D or smaller tiles.\n- Helion generates correct `tl.dot_scaled` patterns for MXFP4 GEMM\n\n### Current Standings (Phase 14 best)\n\n| Kernel | Our Time | Leader | Gap | Status |\n|--------|----------|--------|-----|--------|\n| MLA | 74.3 µs | 4.3 µs | 17x | Phase 14 OR routing + 3D matmul |\n| GEMM | ~23 µs | 9.7 µs | 2.4x | dynamic_mxfp4_quant + gemm_a4w4 |\n| MoE | ~155 µs | 145 µs | 1.07x | Adaptive KSPLIT (API ceiling) |\n\n## Strategy: Compound Layers (Each Enables the Next)\n\n### Layer 1: GEMM — Fix HIP Quant Rounding (HIGH confidence, ~17-26% improvement)\n\n**Problem:** The existing HIP C++ quant kernel uses `floorf(log2f(amax / 6.0f)) + 128.0f` which produces scale bytes ±1 off from aiter's reference. This makes the entire HIP quant output unusable for `gemm_a4w4`.\n\n**Solution:** Port the bit-accurate E8M0 logic from `submission_breakthrough_v9.py:17-22` to HIP C++:\n```c\n// IEEE 754 bitwise: extract exponent, round-up-on-tie\nunsigned int u32 = __float_as_uint(group_max);\nint exponent = (u32 >> 23) & 0xFF;\nint round_up = ((u32 & 0x400000) != 0) && (((u32 & 0x3FFFFF) != 0) || (exponent > 0));\nint exp_val = round_up ? exponent + 1 : exponent;\nscale_byte = (unsigned char)(exp_val - 129 + 127);  // = exp_val - 2\nscale_factor = exp2f((float)(exp_val - 129));\n```\n\n**Pipeline:** HIP quant (~3-5µs) → e8m0_shuffle (~1µs) → gemm_a4w4 ASM (~15µs) = **~19-21µs**\n\n**Why this is compound:** Once HIP quant matches bit-perfectly, it becomes a reusable building block for Layer 3 (fused quant+GEMM). And the compile+load pattern is battle-tested for Layer 2.\n\n### Layer 2: MLA — C++ Bridge to aiter ASM (MEDIUM confidence, potentially 5-10x improvement)\n\n**Problem:** 74µs MLA includes ~50µs of Python overhead (metadata build + dispatch). The ASM `.co` kernels at `/home/runner/aiter/hsa/gfx950/mla/` run in ~4-10µs — the overhead IS the bottleneck.\n\n**Solution:** Use `torch.utils.cpp_extension.load_inline` to build a C++ bridge that calls aiter's ASM kernels directly, bypassing the Python pipeline.\n\n**Step 2a — Probe submission (intelligence gathering):**\nSubmit a diagnostic that maps aiter's exported C++ symbols:\n- `python -c \"import aiter; print(aiter.__file__)\"` → find .so paths\n- `nm -D` on the .so files → list exported symbols containing \"mla\"\n- Print `torch.ops.aiter` registered ops\n- List `/home/runner/aiter/hsa/gfx950/mla/*.co` filenames\n\n**Step 2b — Minimal C++ bridge:**\nIf symbols are exported, build a `load_inline` extension that:\n1. Takes Q, KV, metadata tensors as `torch::Tensor`\n2. Calls the ASM function via `torch::ops::aiter::*` or direct symbol\n3. Returns output — single C++ function call, no Python overhead\n\n**Step 2c — Full integration (no fallback):**\nRoute ALL decode shapes through C++ bridge. If it fails, capture the error, diagnose, and fix — don't fall back. Falling back is failure.\n\n**Why NOT another naive HIP kernel:** The vault experiment proved a naive kernel is 19-100x slower. We should leverage aiter's highly-optimized ASM rather than compete with it. The elegant approach: remove the overhead around the fast kernel, don't rewrite the fast kernel.\n\n### Layer 3: GEMM — Fused Quant+GEMM (LOWER confidence, requires Layer 1)\n\n**Only attempt if Layer 1 succeeds and time permits.** Fuse the HIP quant kernel with the GEMM into a single launch, eliminating the intermediate A_q buffer and the e8m0_shuffle call.\n\nTwo paths:\n- **Path A:** Helion-generated `tl.dot_scaled` Triton kernel with inline quant (simpler but ~68% slower than ASM per Phase 6)\n- **Path B:** HIP C++ kernel using `__builtin_amdgcn_mfma_f32_32x32x64_fp4` (harder but potentially competitive)\n\n### Layer 4: MoE — Fine Block_M Routing (LOW risk, incremental)\n\nOnly if Layers 1-2 complete. Add per-shape `AITER_BLOCK_M` routing based on `estimated_m`:\n- `estimated_m < 16`: block_m=32\n- `16 ≤ estimated_m < 100`: block_m=64\n- `estimated_m ≥ 100`: default CSV\n\n## Implementation Tasks\n\n### Task 1: GEMM — Bit-Accurate HIP Quant (No Fallback)\n- [ ] 1a: Port IEEE 754 E8M0 rounding from `submission_breakthrough_v9.py:17-22` to HIP C++\n- [ ] 1b: Remove `_fallback_quant` 2. **FP4 encoding** — Same story. The e2m1 quantization thresholds and packing can be verified in pure Python against the reference.

3. **aiter source code analysis** — We have aiter installed locally. We can `nm -D` the .so, inspect `torch.ops.aiter`, read the Python source to understand the MLA dispatch path — all without submitting.

4. **HIP C++ syntax** — We can verify the kernel compiles (syntax, types) even if we can't run it on gfx950.

**What we MUST submit for:**
- Actual GPU timing (benchmark)
- Runner-specific paths (`/home/runner/aiter/hsa/gfx950/mla/*.co`)
- Correctness against the runner's test harness

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- Helion generates **correct `tl.dot_scaled` Triton code** for MXFP4 GEMM with inline quantization — confirmed in vault experiment `2026-03-15-helion-triton-codegen-mxfp4.md`
- Phase 6 showed Helion/Triton was ~68% slower than `gemm_a4w4` ASM for the GEMM kernel alone

- For MLA, Helion could potentially generate a `tl.dot_scaled` attention kernel — but the gfx950 VGPR pressure (head_dim=576, not power of 2) makes this very risky
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

If those 4 overheads total >68% of the GEMM time, fusion wins.\n\n**Compound synergy:** Track A's bit-accurate E8M0 math feeds directly into Track B's inline quant logic. Same math, different packaging.\n\n### Track C: MLA — C++ Bridge to aiter ASM (MEDIUM confidence, 5-10x improvement)\n\n**Problem:** 74µs MLA includes ~50µs of Python overhead. ASM kernels run in ~4-10µs.\n\n**Solution:** `torch.utils.cpp_extension.load_inline` C++ bridge calling aiter's ASM directly.\n\n**Local analysis:** Study aiter source code, `nm -D` on .so files, map `torch.ops.aiter` — all doable locally. Only submit a probe for runner-specific paths (`*.co` locations).\n\n**No fallback.** If the bridge fails, capture error, diagnose, fix.\n\n### Track D: MoE — Fine Block_M Routing (LOW risk, incremental)\n\nOnly after Tracks A-C. Per-shape `AITER_BLOCK_M` routing:\n- `estimated_m < 16`: block_m=32\n- `16 ≤ estimated_m < 100`: block_m=64\n- `estimated_m ≥ 100`: default CSV"
}

     1→---
     2→date: 2026-03-15
     3→project: luma-amd-speedrun
     4→status: completed
     5→outcome: success
     6→tags: [experiment, luma-amd-speedrun, helion, triton, mxfp4, codegen]
     7→---
     8→# Helion Generates Correct tl.dot_scaled Triton Code for MXFP4 GEMM
     9→
    10→## Hypothesis
    11→Helion (pytorch/helion v0.3.3) can generate valid Triton kernel code with correct `tl.dot_scaled` scale layouts for MXFP4 GEMM targeting AMD gfx950, which can then be adapted for competition submissions.
    12→
    13→## Method
    14→1. Created isolated CPU-only venv: `python3 -m venv .venv-helion && pip install helion torch --index-url .../cpu`
    15→2. Wrote `@helion.kernel` decorated function with `hl.dot_scaled(a, a_scale, "e2m1", b, b_scale, "e2m1")`
    16→3. Used `bound = kernel.bind(args); bound.set_config(Config(block_sizes=[64,64,64])); bound.to_triton_code()`
    17→4. Read generated code from `/tmp/torchinductor_<user>/` cache
    18→
    19→## Results
    20→- **Code generation: SUCCESS** — valid Triton kernel with correct `tl.dot_scaled` calls
    21→- **Key pattern discovered**: Scale tensors are loaded as full rows `A_scale[tile_m, :]` (all K//32 elements), not per-K-block slices. `tl.dot_scaled` internally selects relevant scales.
    22→- **Format string confirmed**: `'e2m1'` (not `'fp4_e2m1'` or `'mxfp4'`)
    23→- **_RDIM_SIZE**: Scale dimension padded to next power of 2 (224 → 256) with mask
    24→
    25→## Generated Template (inner loop)
    26→```python
    27→for offset_2 in tl.range(0, K_HALF, BLOCK_K):
    28→    indices_2 = offset_2 + tl.arange(0, BLOCK_K)
    29→    load = tl.load(A + (indices_0[:, None] * K_HALF + indices_2[None, :]))
    30→    load_1 = tl.load(A_scale + (indices_0[:, None] * SCALE_K + indices_3[None, :]), mask_3)
    31→    load_2 = tl.load(B + (indices_1[:, None] * K_HALF + indices_2[None, :]))
    32→    load_3 = tl.load(B_scale + (indices_1[:, None] * SCALE_K + indices_3[None, :]), mask_3)
    33→    acc = tl.dot_scaled(load, load_1, 'e2m1', load_2, load_3, 'e2m1', acc=acc)
    34→```
    35→
    36→## Learnings
    37→1. Helion v0.3.3 API: `to_triton_code()` is on `BoundKernel`, not `helion.runtime.debug`
    38→2. Code generation succeeds even without GPU — it fails at the import/compile step (no triton in CPU venv) but the code is already cached
    39→3. Scale indexing uses `tile_m` and full `:` for K dim — NOT `tile_k // 16`
    40→4. Must write kernel to .py file (not stdin) — Helion uses `inspect.getsource()`
    41→
    42→## Follow-up
    43→- Adapt generated code to dynamic shapes (parameterize M, N, K)
    44→- Fuse A-matrix bf16→MXFP4 quantization into the GEMM kernel
    45→- Test on MI355X runner via Popcorn CLI
    46→

     1→# Luma AMD Speedrun: Phase 15 — Compound Custom Kernels
     2→
     3→## Context
     4→
     5→We're at the Python dispatch ceiling across all three kernels. 14 phases of optimization have exhausted every Python-level API path. The only way forward is custom kernels compiled on the runner itself.
     6→
     7→**Critical prior learnings (vault + skills):**
     8→- amdclang++ → .so → ctypes pipeline WORKS (confirmed 2026-03-15)
     9→- Naive HIP attention kernel = **19-100x REGRESSION** — kernel quality >> dispatch overhead
    10→- GEMM HIP quant kernel works but has **wrong E8M0 rounding** (±1 scale mismatch in ~30% of groups)
    11→- Bit-accurate E8M0 logic EXISTS in `submission_breakthrough_v9.py` (IEEE 754 bitwise)
    12→- VGPR budget: `acc[32,512]` fp32 = 256 VGPRs = entire budget. Must use split-D or smaller tiles.
    13→- Helion generates correct `tl.dot_scaled` patterns for MXFP4 GEMM
    14→
    15→### Current Standings (Phase 14 best)
    16→
    17→| Kernel | Our Time | Leader | Gap | Status |
    18→|--------|----------|--------|-----|--------|
    19→| MLA | 74.3 µs | 4.3 µs | 17x | Phase 14 OR routing + 3D matmul |
    20→| GEMM | ~23 µs | 9.7 µs | 2.4x | dynamic_mxfp4_quant + gemm_a4w4 |
    21→| MoE | ~155 µs | 145 µs | 1.07x | Adaptive KSPLIT (API ceiling) |
    22→
    23→## Strategy: Local-First Development on Our Silicon
    24→
    25→**Key assets:**
    26→- **Radeon 8060S** (RDNA 4 iGPU) + **128GB unified LPDDR5X** — run Triton/Helion kernels locally for correctness
    27→- **Helion v0.3.3** installed as wheel — generate `tl.dot_scaled` kernels, iterate locally
    28→- **aiter** installed locally — run `dynamic_mxfp4_quant` reference, inspect MLA dispatch source
    29→- **ROCm toolchain** — compile HIP C++ locally for syntax validation
    30→
    31→Triton/Helion generates architecture-portable IR — we validate correctness locally, submit to MI355X only for final timing. This eliminates the slow submit→wait→read-stdout→fix loop.
    32→
    33→Two parallel GEMM tracks + one MLA track, all validated locally before submission:
    34→
    35→### Track A: HIP C++ Quant → ASM GEMM (HIGH confidence, ~17-26% improvement)
    36→
    37→**Problem:** HIP quant kernel has wrong E8M0 rounding (±1 scale byte in ~30% of groups).
    38→
    39→**Solution:** Port IEEE 754 bitwise E8M0 from `submission_breakthrough_v9.py:17-22` to HIP C++.
    40→```c
    41→// IEEE 754 bitwise: extract exponent, round-up-on-tie
    42→unsigned int u32 = __float_as_uint(group_max);
    43→int exponent = (u32 >> 23) & 0xFF;
    44→int round_up = ((u32 & 0x400000) != 0) && (((u32 & 0x3FFFFF) != 0) || (exponent > 0));
    45→int exp_val = round_up ? exponent + 1 : exponent;
    46→scale_byte = (unsigned char)(exp_val - 2);
    47→scale_factor = exp2f((float)(exp_val - 129));
    48→```
    49→
    50→**Local validation:** Pure Python/numpy implementation of the same math, compared bit-for-bit against `dynamic_mxfp4_quant` output. Math bug → math fix → validate locally → port to HIP C++.
    51→
    52→**Pipeline:** HIP quant (~3-5µs) → e8m0_shuffle (~1µs) → gemm_a4w4 ASM (~15µs) = **~19-21µs**
    53→
    54→### Track B: Helion Fused Quant+GEMM (MEDIUM confidence, potentially better than Track A)
    55→
    56→**Problem:** Track A still has a 3-step pipeline. A single fused kernel eliminates intermediate buffers and launch overhead.
    57→
    58→**Solution:** Use Helion to generate a `tl.dot_scaled` Triton kernel with inline MXFP4 quantization — one kernel launch does quant + GEMM.
    59→
    60→**Local validation:** Run the Helion-generated Triton kernel on our Radeon 8060S for correctness. The `tl.dot_scaled` with `'e2m1'` format is portable — same Triton IR, different backend. Verify output matches reference GEMM result on local GPU.
    61→
    62→**Why this could beat Track A:** Phase 6 showed Triton was ~68% slower for raw GEMM — but that was GEMM-only. A fused kernel eliminates: (1) HIP quant launch, (2) e8m0_shuffle, (3) intermediate A_q allocation, (4) gemm_a4w4 launch overhead. If those 4 overheads total >68% of the GEMM time, fusion wins.
    63→
    64→**Compound synergy:** Track A's bit-accurate E8M0 math feeds directly into Track B's inline quant logic. Same math, different packaging.
    65→
    66→### Track C: MLA — C++ Bridge to aiter ASM (MEDIUM confidence, 5-10x improvement)
    67→
    68→**Problem:** 74µs MLA includes ~50µs of Python overhead. ASM kernels run in ~4-10µs.
    69→
    70→**Solution:** `torch.utils.cpp_extension.load_inline` C++ bridge calling aiter's ASM directly.
    71→
    72→**Local analysis:** Study aiter source code, `nm -D` on .so files, map `torch.ops.aiter` — all doable locally. Only submit a probe for runner-specific paths (`*.co` locations).
    73→
    74→**No fallback.** If the bridge fails, capture error, diagnose, fix.
    75→
    76→### Track D: MoE — Fine Block_M Routing (LOW risk, incremental)
    77→
    78→Only after Tracks A-C. Per-shape `AITER_BLOCK_M` routing:
    79→- `estimated_m < 16`: block_m=32
    80→- `16 ≤ estimated_m < 100`: block_m=64
    81→- `estimated_m ≥ 100`: default CSV
    82→
    83→## Implementation Tasks
    84→
    85→**Tracks A and B run in parallel locally. Submit the winner.**
    86→
    87→### Task 1: Local Math Validation (shared by Tracks A & B)
    88→
    89→- [ ] 1a: Write Python reference: generate random bf16 tensors, run `dynamic_mxfp4_quant`, extract raw E8M0 scale bytes and fp4x2 packed values
    90→- [ ] 1b: Implement IEEE 754 bitwise E8M0 logic in Python (from `submission_breakthrough_v9.py:17-22`), compare against reference — must be 100% bit-match
    91→- [ ] 1c: Implement FP4 e2m1 encoding in Python, compare packed output against reference — must be 100% match
    92→- [ ] 1d: This validated math feeds both Track A (HIP C++) and Track B (Helion inline quant)
    93→
    94→### Task 2: Track A — HIP C++ Quant Pipeline (No Fallback)
    95→
    96→- [ ] 2a: Port validated E8M0 + FP4 math to HIP C++ kernel
    97→- [ ] 2b: Wire as the only pipeline: HIP quant → e8m0_shuffle → gemm_a4w4, no `_fallback_quant`
    98→- [ ] 2c: Submit `--mode test` — high confidence since math was validated locally
    99→- [ ] 2d: Submit `--mode benchmark` — expect ~19-21µs
   100→
   101→### Task 3: Track B — Helion Fused Quant+GEMM (Primary Path)
   102→
   103→**Helion is installed locally as a wheel. Generate, validate, iterate — all on our Radeon 8060S.**
   104→
   105→Helion workflow (from vault experiment `2026-03-15-helion-triton-codegen-mxfp4.md`):
   106→1. Write `@helion.kernel` function with `hl.dot_scaled(a, a_scale, "e2m1", b, b_scale, "e2m1")`
   107→2. `bound = kernel.bind(args); bound.set_config(Config(block_sizes=[64,64,64])); bound.to_triton_code()`

> AGENT

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- `tl.dot_scaled` requires hardware-specific matrix multiply instructions (MFMA on CDNA, WMMA on RDNA). The Radeon 8060S (RDNA 4) may not support the `e2m1` scaled dot product — but Helion's **code generation** step is pure Python and works anywhere. Worst case: we generate and inspect the Triton code locally, validate the math in Python, and only run the actual GPU kernel on MI355X.
- Having two parallel tracks (HIP C++ pipeline vs Helion fusion) is the elegant compound approach — shared math validation (Task 1) feeds both, and we submit whichever wins. No wasted work either way.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

Plan is ready — local-first development, Helion as primary track, no fallbacks, leverage our silicon.
